BACKGROUND: Disulfiram was the first drug approved by FDA for treatment of alcohol dependence way back in 1949, but its use in modern days became limited due to the frequent occurrence of serious adverse reaction particularly with covert use. AIM: To report a rare case of delirium developed in a patient of alcohol dependence syndrome currently abstinent on being treated with disulfiram. METHODS: Clinical interview. RESULTS: We describe a case of a 37-year-old male who was started on disulfiram after the initial detoxification phase. But despite being abstinent the patient suddenly started showing confused behaviour, irrelevant talk, fearfulness, disoriented and hallucinatory behaviour, decreased sleep, restlessness, etc. Disulfiram was withheld immediately and the patient was treated with injectable benzodiazepine in in-patient setting. After discontinuing disulfiram all his symptoms improved dramatically. CONCLUSION: Disulfiram-induced delirium though unusual could be disruptive and severe, warranting immediate intervention. KEY WORDS: Disulfiram, Delirium 02/17: Neurological Soft Signs in Patients with Bipolar Affective Disorder – A case control study Dr. Janhavi Purandare Department of Psychiatry, Mahatma Gandhi Institute of Medical Sciences (MGIMS), Sewagram, Maharashtra. Email: purandarejanhavi1998@gmail.com BACKGROUND &amp; AIMS: Neurological Soft Signs (NSS) are non-localizable neurological abnormalities. They are indicators of neurological impairment and have been found in up to 50% of bipolar affective disorder patients suggesting neuro-developmental basis of illness. The study was carried out to describe NSS in patients with bipolar affective disorder and compare them with soft signs found in their first-degree relatives (FDRs) and healthy controls and to study the association of illness related factors with NSS found in patient. METHODS: The study included 25 patients, 25 FDRs and 25 controls. NSS were assessed using previously validated Neurological Evaluation Scale (NES). RESULTS: Maximum patients showed marked impairment in motor sequencing tasks. Rhythm tapping test was significantly associated with longer total duration of illness. Subscales of NES showed positive correlation with total score. Scores of FDRs were better than patients but worse than that of healthy controls. CONCLUSION: NSS manifest early in life, even before the onset of full-blown bipolar disorder symptoms; and hence, studying these markers may help identify individuals at risk or in the early stages of the disorder. Early detection may lead to timely intervention KEY WORDS: Neurological soft signs (NSS), bipolar affective disorder, Neurological Evaluation Scale (NES), first degree relatives (FDRs) 03/18: Co-occurring bipolar affective disorder and post- traumatic stress disorder: A case report Lt Col SP Panda, MD, DNB, MNAMS; AFMC Pune. Email: drsppanda@gmail.com Introduction: A significant proportion of individuals with Bipolar disorder have experienced trauma – common link with PTSD Case report: This index case with no psychiatric morbidity had presented with of reduced sleep, social withdrawal, suicidal ideas, re-experiencing the traumatic event, hypervigilance and avoidance behavior after witnessing a devastating flood. While on treatment, he developed emergent mania with resolution of symptoms with SDA. Discussion: Handful of case reports of mania in patients of PTSD with depression. Baseline PTSD or Bipolar disorder increased the risk of developing the other illness. Several possibilities including shared genetic or environmental risks may explain these findings. There is negative impact of BPAD and PTSD on symptom burden and overall Quality of Life. Conclusion: It is important to evaluate the patient of BPAD and PTSD for the other condition. It is also important to develop strategies for secondary prevention among patients who experience either condition alone. Key words : BPAD, PTSD 04/30: When Smell Becomes the Shadow: Insights into Olfactory Reference Disorder – A Case Report Dr. Ashish Ranjan Panda Junior Resident, Indlas Hospital, Vijayawada Email : ashish.ranjan97@gmail.com Background : Olfactory Reference Disorder (ORD) is characterized by persistent preoccupation with the belief that one is emitting a perceived foul or offensive body odour or breath that is either unnoticeable or only slightly noticeable to others. Individuals experience excessive self-consciousness about the perceived odour, often accompanied by ideas of reference. Clinical Case : A 26-year-old male presented with complaints of visiting multiple dentists and otolaryngologists due to a perceived bad smell from his mouth and nose. He reported brushing his teeth four to five times a day and cleaning his tongue repeatedly, feeling that people around him were annoyed and constantly judging him for the past seven years. On physical examination, the patient was thin and malnourished. Mental Status Examination (MSE) revealed a dull mood, death wishes, and preoccupation with the bad smell. MRI of the brain and EEG were normal. On the Yale-Brown Obsessive Compulsive Scale – Olfactory Reference Syndrome (YBOCS-ORS), the patient scored 36, on Hamilton Depression Rating Scale (HAM-D), he reported a score of 26. He was prescribed fluoxetine 40 mg along with a low dose of olanzapine. Cognitive Behavioural Therapy (CBT) was initiated to complement the remission process. Conclusion: ORD is often under-recognized in patients seeking medical attention. It is important for physicians to assess the patient for relevant symptoms, inform them about the potential diagnosis, and refer them to a mental health professional for further evaluation and treatment. Key words: Olfactory reference disorder, preoccupation, fluoxetine, CBT 05/37: Seeing the unseen: a case report on Charles Bonnet Syndrome Dr Geethu V S 1 , Dr Keerthy Reji 2 1 Second year PG, 2 Assistant professor Department of Psychiatry, Sree Uthradom Thirunal Academy of Medical Sciences, Thiruvananthapuram, Kerala. Email: geethuvs93@gmail.com INTRODUCTION: Charles Bonnet Syndrome (CBS) is characterized by visual hallucinations which are “released” in visual cortex, as brain compensates for reduced input due to significant visual impairment. Insight is retained but it’s very distressing to the patient and may lead to depressive disorder or frank psychosis. CASE DESCRIPTION: 22 year old female with no previous psychiatric history and recently diagnosed with retinitis pigmentosa. She presented with complaints of seeing terrifying images, low mood and insomnia. On physical examination she had reduced visual acuity, MSE revealed visual hallucinations, dysphoric mood, preoccupation regarding loss of vision, visual hallucination, ideas of worthlessness and helplessness with grade 4 insight. She was managed with antipsychotics, antidepressants, supportive psychotherapy and family psychoeducation with concurrent treatment from ophthalmology department. CONCLUSION : Psychoeducating about CBS with appropriate use of antipsychotics &amp; antidepressants significantly reduced the patient’s distress with symptomatic improvement and better quality of life. KEYWORDS: Charles Bonnet Syndrome, Visual hallucination, Depression 06/39: Paradoxical reaction to benzodiazepines - a rare case report Dr. Sindhu Maddineni Department of Psychiatry, Dr. Patnam Mahender Reddy Institute of Medical Sciences, Chevella, Telangana. Email: bharghavichowdary@gmail.com Introduction: Benzodiazepines are commonly used for alcohol withdrawal and agitation but can rarely (&lt;1%) cause paradoxical reactions like heightened agitation or hostility. This report explores such a reaction to lorazepam in a hospitalised patient. Case description: A 42-year-old man with fractures, alcohol abuse, and sleep disturbances was referred to psychiatry with alcohol withdrawal (CIWA score: 14). He was given lorazepam (4 mg stat, 8 mg in divided doses) and thiamine but developed severe agitation, requiring transfer to critical care. Despite increasing lorazepam and adding haloperidol, his agitation persisted until these were discontinued. Promethazine and later chlordiazepoxide effectively stabilised him, allowing transfer back to the ward. Discussion: This case highlights a rare paradoxical reaction to lorazepam, worsening agitation instead of relieving it. Switching to chlordiazepoxide and promethazine resolved symptoms. Prompt recognition and treatment adjustment are essential for recovery. This study is aimed to describe three cases of Neuropsychiatric Comorbidity in Titubation. Methods: Case series Case 1: Mrs ABC, 50-year-old female, K/C/O Moderate Depression with Somatic Syndrome and Titubation with Low Haemoglobin, Hypothyroidism, increased ESR and Hypertension. Patient was advised T. Escitalopram 10mg, T. Clonazepam 0.25mg and T. Propranolol 40mg h/s OD. Case 2: Mrs XYZ, 60-year-old female, K/C/O Acute Psychosis and Titubation presented with Delirium. Clozapine 50mg ½-1-2, T. Propranolol 40mg TDS, T. Melatonin 3mg TDS, T. Ecosprin 150mg OD, T. Atorvastatin 40mg H/S OD and T. Levodopa+ Carbidopa (100+25)mg ½ TDS. Etizolam 0.5mg bd &amp; SOS. Results: All three women had Psychiatric Disorders, Low Haemoglobin, Thyroid Dysfunction, Hypertension and Post menopause with Titubation. Conclusion: This needs further studies on role of Low Haemoglobin, Thyroid Dysfunction, Hypertension, Post menopause, Neurological Comorbidity and Titubation. Additional role of Inflammation and Autoimmune Phenomena needs further studies. Key words: Titubation, Neuropsychiatry, Somatic Syndrome, Psychosis. 09/50: Psychiatric manifestations in patient of acute kidney injury Dr. Tayade Harshali Junior Resident-2, Department of Psychiatry, BJGMC &amp; Sassoon General Hospital, Pune Maharashtra, India. Email: dr22372tayade@gmail.com Background &amp; Aims: Renal failure is associated with many subtle and several distinct alterations in neuropsychiatric function. Methods : 73-year-old male with sudden onset behavioural disturbances was admitted. He was started on low dose antipsychotics. After 2 days, his general medical condition worsened with abnormal LFT, RFT. USG (A+P) was s/o renal parenchymal disease and cystitis. Results: Psychotropics were withheld and the patient was transferred to the medicine ward where he was treated. Psychiatry review revealed improvement in his behavioural disturbances. Conclusion: A collaborative liaison between the nephrologist and psychiatrist is required to identify and treat the neuro psychiatric disturbances arising in a case of renal dysfunction. Email: aghicletus@gmail.com BACKGROUND AND OBJECTIVES: Persistent Genital Arousal Disorder (PGAD) is a rare condition causing spontaneous genital arousal without sexual desire. Limited awareness complicates diagnosis and treatment, underscoring the need for prompt recognition and effective intervention. CASE :43-year-old married woman premorbidly well-adjusted, presented with apprehension, stress, and social avoidance. She reported persistent genital arousal with orgasmic sensations unrelated to sexual arousal or desire leading to concerns about others noticing her emotional state resulting in avoidance of social situations. The patient with controlled T2DM for 5 years exhibited anxiety in the MSE. Investigations were normal except for ultrasound findings of adenomyosis and left ovarian unilocular cyst. CONCLUSION: PGAD symptoms often make reporting difficult for patients emphasizing the need for greater awareness to enable accurate diagnosis and effective treatment. KEY WORDS: PGAD - Persistent Genital Arousal Disorder, FSD - Female Sexual Dysfunction 11/58: Asperger’s syndrome with OCS with Ocular epilepsy- A Rare Case Dr. Bhawana Thakur Department of Psychiatry, Maulana Azad Medical College (MAMC), New Delhi, India Email: dr.bhanuthakur.98@gmail.com Introduction: Asperger’s Syndrome (AS) classified under autism spectrum disorders (ASDs), involves social interaction, communication and repetitive behaviour challenges. Aim: To present a case of Asperger’s Syndrome with ocular epilepsy. Case Report: A 25year old male BBA graduate from New Delhi, exhibited minimal social communication, poor eye contact and preference for strict routine and solitude since childhood. He excelled academically but showed minimal social interaction later got diagnosed with Asperger’s Syndrome. Later parents noticed multiple episodes of eyelid jerks lasting few seconds to minutes, particularly in bright light, not suppressible, later prompting neurological consultation that confirmed ocular epilepsy and started on antiepileptics. In 2016, he developed obsessive fears of negative outcomes, engaging in compulsive behaviours like asking his mother to touch him thrice, responded well to SSRIs and benzodiazepines. Discussion: Comorbid epilepsy in AS is rare with 4% prevalence compared to 77% and 94% in Childhood Disintegrative Disorder and Rett Syndrome, respectively. Carminati et al. observed that antiepileptic adjustments improved life quality in both ASD patients with OCD, with or without epilepsy. Conclusion: Given the rarity of epilepsy, particularly ocular epilepsy in AS, misdiagnoses as tics are common, delaying treatment. This rare case highlights importance of recognizing possible epilepsy in Asperger’s patients. Key words : Asperger’s syndrome, Obsessive compulsive symptoms, Ocular epilepsy 12/59: Unmasking psychosis: a case report on acute psychosis triggered by ectopic parathyroid adenoma Dr. Keerthy Reji, Dr. S. Anoop K Raj Department of psychiatry, Sree Uthradom Thirunal Academy Of Medical Science, Thiruvananthapuram, Kerala. Email: dr.sanoopkraj@gmail.com Background: Hypercalcemia is known to cause neuropsychiatric dysfunction including mood and cognitive changes and rarely, acute psychosis. Primary hyperparathyroidism is a disease with multisystemic and heterogeneous manifestations, characterized by underlying high parathormone concentration. Case: A case of a 54-year-old female who presented with a history of acute onset of confusion, fearfulness, auditory hallucination, and persecutory delusions for two days. The patient was admitted to the psychiatry ward and started on antipsychotics and benzodiazepines, and baseline investigations showed elevated serum calcium, and was treated with I/V fluids. The psychotic symptoms of patient resolved with decrease in serum calcium level, but it was difficult to maintain normal calcium level and further evaluation revealed an ectopic parathyroid adenoma on a radioisotope technetium-99m Sestamibi (MIBI) scan, which was determined to be the primary cause of her hypercalcemia. Conclusion: In this case, we emphasize the importance of recognizing psychosis as an initial presentation of ectopic parathyroid adenoma induced hypercalcemia, and ruling out organic aetiologias before diagnosing a primary psychosis can be lifesaving. Keywords: Psychosis, Ectopic Parathyroid. 13/60: Mania in an Adolescent: A Case Report Dr Aboobacker Alphy K H 1 , Dr Shashidhar Bhat 2 , Dr Akshay Jadhav 3 , Brig (Dr) P S Bhat (Retd) 4 Department of Psychiatry, DBVPRMC, Loni, Maharashtra. Email: alphyaboobacker2001@gmail.com Background Adolescence is marked by rapid physical, psychological, and social changes, making it vulnerable to stressors. Adolescent bipolar disorder often goes unrecognized, leading to academic, social, and family dysfunction, along with increased suicide risks. Case Summary A 13-year-old girl from a rural, lower socioeconomic background was brought with a 10-day history of hyperactivity, aggressive behavior, decreased sleep and appetite, mood lability, grandiose ideas, and childlike behavior. General and neurological examinations were unremarkable. MSE revealed a non-cooperative, conscious girl with labile affect and attention deficits. MRI was normal. She was treated with Olanzapine (up to 20 mg) and Lorazepam, showing significant improvement within 15 days. Conclusion Pediatric bipolar disorder differs from adult-onset in symptoms, duration, comorbidities, and management, highlighting the need for early recognition and treatment. Key words: Mania, Adolescent. 14/64,85: Genetic Correlates of Autism in Females: Exploring the Female Protective Effect and Fear Expression Ms. Aardhraya Nair &amp; Dr. Shabbir Amanullah Queen’s University, Kingston Ontario, Canada. Email: aardnair@gmail.com &amp; shabbir.amanullah@gmail.com Background &amp; Aims: Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) shows sex-based disparities, with males diagnosed 3-4 times more often than females. The “Female Protective Effect” (FPE) suggests that females may require a higher genetic load to exhibit ASD symptoms. This study investigates how genetic factors, FPE, and fear expression impact ASD in females, aiming to support more accurate, sex-specific diagnostics. Methods: A literature search across PubMed and OMNI identified genetic and behavioral studies. A scoping review approach was used to integrate findings on ASD-related genetic variants in females. Results: FPE is supported, with key genetic variants in neurodevelopment and synaptic function playing a role in the protective mechanism. FPE is associated with reduced fear responses, suggesting that genetic protection may influence emotional regulation and modify ASD symptoms. Conclusion: These findings emphasize FPE’s role in shaping ASD’s genetic and behavioral profile in females. Key words: Female Protective Effect (FPE), Genetic Factors, Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD), Fear Expression, Sex Differences 15/70,84: Barriers to diagnosing ASD in females using the ADOS-2: A graduate’s perspective Katherine Rachel Hoffer Department of Psychiatry, Queen’s University, Kingston, Ontario, Canada. Email: 19krh3@queensu.ca Background &amp; Aims: Findings in research over the past decade have shown significant sex differences in autism spectrum disorder (ASD). The recognition of gender discrepancies raises concerns about ASD assessment tools, which were developed based on the typical male autism phenotype, and whether these are actually sex-friendly gold-standard tools. This study addresses this literature gap by discussing a graduate’s perspective of the ADOS-II, focusing on the hurdles the ADOS-II presents to female diagnosis. Methods: To find relevant articles to compare with the graduate’s ideas, a literature search was conducted on two databases, Omni and PubMed. To be included, the articles needed to present perspectives on autism diagnostic tools concerning barriers to female diagnosis. With few articles meeting this criterion, papers discussing diagnostic tools more generally were included, even if the ADOS-II was not explicitly referenced. Results: The search yielded 240 results; 192 results after removing duplicates. After reviewing titles and abstracts, 65 full-text articles were considered, identifying six suitable articles. Conclusion: The graduate’s perspective will be compared to these articles. Overarching themes and ideas for future research will also be discussed. Key words: Autism Spectrum Disorder, ADOS-2, Gender bias, Diagnostic barriers, Autism in females 16/72: Title: Diagnostic Dilemma in Neurocysticercosis with Comorbid Dissociative Disorder Dr. Sudeepti singh Department of Psychiatry, BJ government medical college, Pune. Email: sudeeptisingh25@gmail.com Introduction: Neurocysticercosis, a parasitic brain infection caused by Taenia solium, often presents with neurological symptoms, complicating diagnosis when psychiatric symptoms co-occur. Dissociative disorders can mimic neurological conditions, creating challenges in overlapping physical and psychological presentations. Case details: A 28-year-old married female presented with recurrent headaches and episodes of loss of consciousness following family conflict. A CT scan revealed a calcific nodule (9.8 mm x 7.4 mm) consistent with grade 4 neurocysticercosis. Her symptoms suggested stress-induced dissociative/conversion disorder. Amitriptyline improved her symptoms, and levetiracetam addressed potential seizure-like activity. She relapsed after another psychosocial stressor, raising questions about the primary cause. Discussion: This case highlights the complexity of diagnosing coexisting neurocysticercosis and dissociative disorder. Differentiating neurological from psychological origins is crucial for effective treatment. Conclusion: Neurocysticercosis with comorbid dissociative disorder requires a multidisciplinary approach for optimal management. CASE DETAILS: A 28-year-old woman presented with low mood, death wishes, and neurological symptoms, including difficulty walking, tremors, speech disturbances, and involuntary jerky movements. Case History A 26-year-old male presented to emergency after amputating his penis. He experienced delusions of persecution and auditory hallucinations, commanding him to sacrifice his penis as penance for perceived sins. Case History A 40-year-old male ingested a blade, driven by delusions of a family conspiracy to kill him. These delusions, persisting for years, worsened in 15 days due to non-adherence to antipsychotics. His paranoia disrupted functioning. Key words: Self harm, delusional disorder. 23/83: Treatment Challenges in a Case of Schizoaffective Disorder with Comorbid Breast Cancer Dr. Sakshi Bankar 1 , Dr. Apoorva Bachhav 2 , Dr. Avinash Karde 3 , Dr. Niteen Abhivant 4 Department of Psychiatry, B J Government Medical College Pune. Email: sakshi.bankar123@gmail.com Introduction : Managing schizoaffective disorder with comorbid breast cancer is challenging due to antipsychotic-induced hyperprolactinemia, a potential poor prognostic factor for breast cancer. Case Details : A 58-year-old female with a 22-year history of schizoaffective disorder presented with psychotic and manic symptoms. Three months prior, she was diagnosed with infiltrating intraductal carcinoma of the breast. She had been on risperidone (6 mg/day), which elevated prolactin (32 ng/ml). Risperidone was replaced with olanzapine (10 mg/day), and aripiprazole (5 mg/day) was added to lower prolactin. Valproate (1000 mg/day) was continued for mood stabilization. Discussion : Antipsychotics, especially risperidone, can increase prolactin, affecting breast cancer outcomes. Switching to olanzapine and adding aripiprazole helped manage both psychiatric symptoms and prolactin levels. Conclusion : Careful antipsychotic selection and regular monitoring are crucial when treating schizoaffective disorder in patients with comorbid breast cancer. Keywords : Schizoaffective disorder, Breast cancer, Hyperprolactinemia, Antipsychotics 24/87: Persistent Delirium Tremens In Wernicke’s Encephalopathy: A Case Report Dr. Shubhamdeep Patel 1 , Dr. Bipasha Karak 2 , Ms. G Swarnameenaa 3 , Dr. Udayan Bhakat 4 , Dr. Sourav Khanra 5 1 Junior Resident, 2 Junior Resident, 3 MPhil trainee, 4 Senior Resident, 5 Professor Dept. of Psychiatry, Central Institute of Psychiatry, Ranchi. INTRODUCTION : Delirium Tremens maybe refractory to standard benzodiazepine doses. Further strategies include high doses/combinations of benzodiazepines, phenobarbitone, fentanyl, and propofol. Wernicke’s Encephalopathy should also be considered. CASE SUMMARY: Patient presented with complaints of alcohol intake for 24 years, tremulousness, seizures and confused behaviour. Physical examination revealed fine tremors, palpable liver margin, tongue ulcers and positive Romberg’s sign. Blood tests revealed deranged LFT. USG showed Grade 2 fatty liver and CT Brain showed cerebral cortical atrophy. Provisional diagnosis was kept as F10.24 + F10.41+ F10.6 + ALD. Tab. Lorazepam started and hiked till 24mg and Tab. Sodium valproate was started considering recurrent seizures, along with Thiamine(1500mg/day) supplementation. Patient worsened whenever Lorazepam was tapered and ultimately switched to Tab. Clonazepam 3.5mg, with good response. Neuropsychological assessment showed dysfunction in motor, writing and memory domains. Although not classified as a psychiatric disorder, it has gained attention due to its links with obesity. Etiological factors include brain–gut–microbiome changes, genetic polymorphisms (dopamine, opioid, leptin), and altered brain connectivity. Risk factors include depression, anxiety, emotional dysregulation, impulsivity, and stress-induced comfort eating. Management strategies encompass lifestyle changes, cognitive behavioral therapy, pharmacotherapy, cognitive training, psychobiotics, low-carb ketogenic diets, self-help groups, virtual reality, and neuromodulation techniques. Addressing FA is critical for sustainable obesity management. Preventive efforts should focus on early identification in at-risk adolescents, promoting healthy eating habits, and regulating access to hyperpalatable foods in youth settings. Responsibility lies with the food industry, governments, and society to implement measures curbing FA and its impact. Socio-demographic and clinical data were collected, and outcome variables IBS-Severity Scoring System(IBS-SSS) and IBS-Quality of Life (IBS-QOL) were assessed at baseline, 2, 4, and 8 weeks. INTRODUCTION: Cyclic Vomiting Syndrome (CVS) is a disorder characterized by recurrent, stereotypic episodes of incapacitating nausea, vomiting and other symptoms, separated by intervals of comparative wellness. CASE PRESENTATION: A 19-year-old female presented to the OPD with chief complaints of recurrent episodes of vomiting since the year 2016, which was precipitated by an episode of sexual abuse (non-penetrative) by her distant cousin. Around 4-5 months following sexual abuse, patient started having episodes of vomiting. A day or two before the episode, she would reduce her food intake and would report that she would feel nauseous if she eats. A couple of days later, she would start having vomiting episodes, which would be liquid in consistency predominantly after having food. The episodes would last for 3-4 days initially, with a frequency of an attack every 2-3 months. They visited multiple doctors after this, where she would be treated symptomatically and sent back home. DISCUSSION: CVS is usually characterized by 3 phases: Prodromal, Emetic and Recovery phase. The prodrome of a cyclic vomiting episode is important because it allows for attempts at aborting the emetic phase. The most common symptom is Nausea, followed by Epigastric pain, fatigue and weakness. The emetic phase is characterized by persistence of many prodromal symptoms plus severe, persistent nausea and vomiting. CONCLUSION: CVS affects patients of all ages. CVS is a disorder in which the patient’s emotional wellbeing strongly influences the success of management. INTRODUCTION: The treatment of opioid use disorder (OUD) in adolescents is a growing challenge, given their unique developmental vulnerabilities and limited approved pharmacological options. METHODS: Three adolescents aged 14-17 years with moderate-to-severe OUD were treated in a tertiary care centre in eastern India. RESULTS: Case 1- A 14-year-old male with codeine dependence and generalised anxiety disorder who achieved full remission with a 10 µg/hour buprenorphine patch and anxiolytic therapy. Case 3- A 17-year-old male with heroin use and polysubstance experimentation, initially responded to 10 µg/hour dosing but relapsed after two weeks, though reporting reduced use. All cases were put on Antagonist management with Naltrexone 50mg OD PO as long-term management therapy after detoxification phase. All patients showed significant reduction in withdrawal symptoms, with caregiver involvement and structured follow-up proving crucial for treatment adherence. CONCLUSION: Buprenorphine transdermal patches appear to be a viable and safe option for detoxification in adolescents with OUD, particularly in resource-constrained settings. The co- management of psychiatric comorbidities and comprehensive psychosocial support are critical for optimising outcomes. Email: shifahi.taj97@gmail.com Background &amp; Aim Autism Spectrum Disorders (ASD) are neurodevelopmental disorders characterised by persistent difficulties in social communication, restricted and repetitive behaviours. Recent reports suggest that inborn errors of metabolism like Phenylketonuria are associated with ASD but have been reported in few studies. Case: A 3 years old male child, 2 nd born out of a second-degree consanguineous marriage was evaluated for delay in motor and speech developmental milestones. The child was diagnosed to have hypothyroidism around 6 months of age and was started on regular thyroxine supplements. The child was evaluated for features of hypopigmented hair, developmental delay. Tandem mass spectrometry showed increased blood phenylalanine levels and was diagnosed with phenylketonuria – biopterin cofactor defect. MRI brain showed mild diffusion restriction in bilateral caudate nucleus, putamen. Whole genome sequencing suggested homozygous, likely pathogenic variant of PKU. He was started on protein and phenylalanine restricted diet and supplemented with levocarnitine. At 3 years and 6 months of age, the child had difficulty in socialization, communication and had hyperactivity features. He was interested in solitary play, spinning toys, preference to play only on wet surfaces and behavioural problems like hitting others and screaming. On evaluating with Childhood Autism Rating Scale, he scored 34 suggestive of severe autism. Oral Atomoxetine was given for 6 months. Child was followed up on monthly basis with speech and occupational therapists and nutritionist. At 6 years of age, speech and behavioural disturbances improved significantly with a fall in CARS score to mild autism range. Discussion Delayed diagnosis of PKU is known to cause intellectual impairment and higher association with severe autistic features. Thus, early diagnosis and intervention accounts for reasonably good outcomes. Conclusion These treatable metabolic conditions like PKU can be considered in the diagnostic approach of patients with syndromic ASD due to high chances for early intervention and better outcomes. Methods: A twenty-five-year-old-male patient presented to a tertiary-care outpatient hospital following the considerable discomfort experienced in relation to a vocal tic. At intake, the vocalization took the form of the patient initially pushing his tongue out of his mouth, extending and tensing his neck toward his right, and then exhaling. He would continue to exhale until he experienced a build-up like sensation in his larynx after which he released a final exhale. The tic was reported as being preceded by a premonitory sensation which was experienced in the larynx. If the tic was not experienced as ‘just right’, he would re-do the movement until satisfaction. Thus, causing considerable impairment when engaged in verbal conversations. Tic suppressibility was possible through distraction techniques; however, these were ineffective in scenarios in which a heightened degree of stress was experienced. Results: Within a six-week treatment window and as measured at pre- and post-intervention, a fifty-percent self-reported tic management improvement score was indicated. Conclusions: The treatment of a complex vocal tic in GTS can be complex, but there is good evidence indicating toward the effectiveness of habit-reversal. Email: positivedavis2009@gmail.com Background: Lithium is considered the gold standard in the treatment of bipolar disorder over decades. Cardiac side-effects are not common though a variety of ECG changes have been described at therapeutic and toxic serum levels. Case: A 67-year-old lady with bipolar disorder on treatment with lithium remained well and asymptomatic for 23 years. Following a two-day history of fever, she was found to have pulse rate of 42 to 49 beats per minute with no associated symptoms or examination findings. Family history of regular tobacco use in father and opioid dependence in son and well-adjusted premorbid personality. She presented with complaints of difficulty in sleep pertaining to initiation and frequent mid night awakenings, headaches, and irritability and reported consuming milk tea (Indian chai) regularly for 36 years. Assessment revealed daily consumption of tea leaves amounted to 80–90 g (equivalent to approximately 1–3 g of caffeine) with a progressive increase in frequency and quantity of consumption. Attempts to reduce intake caused withdrawal symptoms, relieved by consuming caffeinated beverages with use persisting despite recurrent epigastric pain, sleep disturbances, and impaired control over consumption, such as drinking tea during the night and during religious fasts like Ramadan. Results: A diagnosis of caffeine withdrawal and harmful pattern of use due to caffeine was made as per ICD 11 guidelines for excessive milk tea consumption. Patient also fulfils the criteria for caffeine use disorder as per DSM 5. The patient was treated symptomatically, incorporating non-pharmacological measures such as maintaining a caffeine diary and brief intervention. Conclusion: Many caffeine-containing products, such as tea leaves, in India do not disclose caffeine content or safety limits. Considering the harmful effects, legal availability, and rising use of caffeine products, raising awareness of caffeine’s addictive potential is essential. This study compares RNFL and MT in acute schizophrenic patients with age- and sex-matched healthy controls. Methods: Twenty acute schizophrenic patients and 20 matched controls underwent RNFL and MT measurement via spectral-domain Optical Coherence Tomography following clinical and ocular assessments. Email: vamsidhark99@gmail.com Background: Alexander Disease (AD) is a rare neurodegenerative leukodystrophy caused by an autosomal dominant mutation in the GFAP gene. This mutation uniquely affects astrocyte cells. Infantile AD, the most common form, typically presents in the first two years of life with symptoms including progressive psychomotor retardation, loss of developmental milestones, macrocephaly, convulsions, and ataxia, along with characteristic frontal white matter abnormalities on MRI. Case Report: A 5-year-old male presented with difficulties in walking and speaking, along with severe intellectual disability. Genetic testing confirmed a heterozygous missense variation in exon 4 of the GFAP gene on chromosome 17, leading to a definitive diagnosis of Alexander Disease. Conclusion: GFAP gene analysis serves as a crucial marker for diagnosing infantile AD based on clinical features and MRI findings, even in the absence of macrocephaly. Conclusion: Timely identification of psychiatric conditions requiring intensive care, along with an integrated approach by critical care psychiatrists and intensivists, can significantly reduce morbidity and mortality in psychiatric ICUs. Col Virendra Vikram Singh 1 , Maj Parveen Kumar, Maj Sandeep HoD, Psychiatry Department, Base Hospital, Barrackpore Email: virvik19@gmail.com Stroke is not often suspected in young patients. The prevalence of stroke in young adults aged 20 to 34 is 0.4%. Stroke is associated or followed by psychotic symptoms in some cases. We present a case of 34-year-old male who developed acute psychosis characterised by perplexed and fearful affect, delusion of persecution and reference, and transient third person auditory hallucinations. Alcohol Dependence Syndrome (ADS), a complex dynamic process is a serious public health problem and a central concept in the World Health Organization classification of alcoholism. This syndrome involves physical signs in addition to psychological symptoms that contribute to distress and psychological discomfort. Several neurobiological and environmental factors influence motivation to drink leading to an altered relationship between the person and his/her way of drinking, which becomes a feedback behaviour comprising much more than tolerance and abstinence. Various cognitive, personality and situational factors have been found to be related to impulsivity and aggression. Additionally, it is well established that aggression and impulsivity are more likely to occur in the context of alcohol use. Impulsivity is defined as acting without forethought, lack of concern about the consequence of one’s own behaviour, unable to control, lacking willpower to give up his or her temptation. The discussion has also been verified: impulsivity as measured in prospective studies has been shown to envisage the development of alcohol use disorders. There are well-established links between impulsivity and alcohol use in humans and other model organisms; though, the etiological nature of these associations remains unclear. Additionally, there is prosperity of research about the links connecting alcohol use and executive functioning (EF) - higher-order cognitive constructs, concerned with the self-regulation of goal-directed behavior which is therefore highly relevant for the avoidance of maladaptive behaviors. Research has suggested a potential role for social problem solving as a mediator between impulsivity and aggression. Hence, the purpose and implication of this study is to understand the role of impulsivity and executive functioning associated with Alcohol dependence and likely areas of intervention. 361: Sharper Than a Dog′s Nose: The Power of Guilt in Persistent Olfactory Delusions Dr. Mohamed Hakkim, Dr. Nirmal S.R., Dr. Muralitharan. Dr. Jai Singh 1. Junior Resident 2. Prof and HOD 3. Professor, Dhanalakshmi Srinivasan Medical College Hospital, Siruvachur, Perambalur, Tamil Nadu Background: Persistent delusional disorder is a rare psychiatric condition characterized by the presence of fixed delusions lasting for an extended duration. Olfactory delusions, although uncommon, can significantly impair the quality of life. Case Report: 57-year-old male came with a 20-year history of delusions involving foul smell following an unusual and distressing experience. Collateral history was collected to corroborate the delusional beliefs and assess psychosocial impairments. The patient’s belief system, behaviour patterns, and duration of illness were analysed in the context of cultural influences and stigma. The patient attributed the foul smell originating from his nose and mouth to a long-standing infection following sexual interactions with a dog during his time in Saudi Arabia. He exhibited frequent mouth washing, social withdrawal, and preoccupation with his delusional beliefs. Despite no confirmatory medical findings of infection or physical abnormalities, the delusion persisted, significantly impairing his social and occupational functioning. Conclusion: This case underscores the interplay of culture, guilt, and stigma in the development and persistence of olfactory delusions. Early intervention and a culturally sensitive therapeutic approach are critical for improving outcomes in such cases. Key words: delusions, olfactory, culture Body dysmorphic disorder in type 1 diabetes Dr Anshuli Tiwari 1 , Dr Mazher Ali 2 , Dr Minhajzafar Nasirabadi 3 , Dr Bipin Sethi 4 , Dr Vinoothna Chowdary Athaluri 5 1. Post Graduate, Dept of Psychiatry, Deccan College of Medical Sciences 2. Assistant Professor, Dept Of Psychaitry, Neelima Institue of Medical Sciences 3. Professor and HOD, Dept of Psychiatry, Deccan College of Medical Sciences 4. Consultant Endocrinologist, Care Hospitals Email : anshulitiwari92@gmail.com Background &amp; Aims : Body Dysmorphic Disorder (BDD) is a mental health condition characterised by excessive concern over perceived physical flaws. Its prevalence among individuals with Type 1 Diabetes (T1D) remains largely unexplored. Considering the unique psychological and physical challenges faced by T1D patients, it is important to assess the potential impact of BDD within this group. Methods: This cross-sectional study was conducted among patients attending an endocrinology clinic. Participants were screened for BDD using the Body Image Disturbance Questionnaire (BIDQ), and clinical interviews were conducted to assess whether they met the DSM-V criteria for BDD. Results: Among the 140 participants, 57% expressed concerns about some aspect of their appearance. The prevalence of BDD, meeting DSM-V criteria, was 19%, notably higher than the 1.7–2.9% prevalence in the general USA population. Of those diagnosed, 52% were female and 48% male, with a mean age of 21 years. Females were primarily dissatisfied with their weight, hair, skin, thighs, and teeth, whereas males expressed concerns over tummy fat, muscle build, and eyes. Conclusion: This study reveals a significant prevalence of BDD in individuals with T1D, emphasising the need for targeted psychological support and interventions to address body image concerns in this population. Keywords: BDD, Type 1 Diabetes, BDIQ
